This &gt; administration will focus on three interlocking strategies for the &gt; region: &gt; &gt; * countries with major impact on their neighborhood such as South &gt; Africa, Nigeria, Kenya, and Ethiopia are anchors for regional engagement &gt; and require focused attention; &gt; &gt; * coordination with European allies and international institutions is &gt; essential for constructive conflict mediation and successful peace &gt; operations; and &gt; &gt; * Africa&#x27;s capable reforming states and sub-regional organizations &gt; must be strengthened as the primary means to address transnational &gt; threats on a sustained basis. &gt; &gt; &gt; Ultimately the path of political and economic freedom presents the &gt; surest route to progress in sub-Saharan Africa, where most wars are &gt; conflicts over material resources and political access often tragically &gt; waged on the basis of ethnic and religious difference. The transition to &gt; the African Union with its stated commitment to good governance and a &gt; common responsibility for democratic political systems offers &gt; opportunities to strengthen democracy on the continent. &gt; &gt; V. Prevent Our Enemies from Threatening Us, Our Allies, and Our Friends &gt; with Weapons of Mass Destruction &gt; &gt; &gt; &quot;The gravest danger to freedom lies at the crossroads of radicalism and &gt; technology. When the spread of chemical and biological and nuclear &gt; weapons, along with ballistic missile technology -- when that occurs, &gt; even weak states and small groups could attain a catastrophic power to &gt; strike great nations. Our enemies have declared this very intention, and &gt; have been caught seeking these terrible weapons. They want the &gt; capability to blackmail us, or to harm us, or to harm our friends -- and &gt; we will oppose them with all our power.&quot; &gt; &gt; &gt; &gt; &gt; &gt; President Bush &gt; West Point, New York &gt; June 1, 2002 &gt; &gt; &gt; &gt; The nature of the Cold War threat required the United States -- with our &gt; allies and friends -- to emphasize deterrence of the enemy&#x27;s use of &gt; force, producing a grim strategy of mutual assured destruction. With the &gt; collapse of the Soviet Union and the end of the Cold War, our security &gt; environment has undergone profound transformation. &gt; &gt; Having moved from confrontation to cooperation as the hallmark of our &gt; relationship with Russia, the dividends are evident: an end to the &gt; balance of terror that divided us; an historic reduction in the nuclear &gt; arsenals on both sides; and cooperation in areas such as &gt; counterterrorism and missile defense that until recently were &gt; inconceivable. &gt; &gt; But new deadly challenges have emerged from rogue states and terrorists. &gt; None of these contemporary threats rival the sheer destructive power &gt; that was arrayed against us by the Soviet Union. However, the nature and &gt; motivations of these new adversaries, their determination to obtain &gt; destructive powers hitherto available only to the world&#x27;s strongest &gt; states, and the greater likelihood that they will use weapons of mass &gt; destruction against us, make today&#x27;s security environment more complex &gt; and dangerous. &gt; &gt; In the 1990s we witnessed the emergence of a small number of rogue &gt; states that, while different in important ways, share a number of &gt; attributes. These states: &gt; &gt; * brutalize their own people and squander their national resources &gt; for the personal gain of the rulers; &gt; &gt; * display no regard for international law, threaten their neighbors, &gt; and callously violate international treaties to which they are party; &gt; &gt; * are determined to acquire weapons of mass destruction, along with &gt; other advanced military technology, to be used as threats or offensively &gt; to achieve the aggressive designs of these regimes; &gt; &gt; * sponsor terrorism around the globe; and &gt; &gt; * reject basic human values and hate the United States and everything &gt; for which it stands. &gt; &gt; &gt; At the time of the Gulf War, we acquired irrefutable proof that Iraq&#x27;s &gt; designs were not limited to the chemical weapons it had used against &gt; Iran and its own people, but also extended to the acquisition of nuclear &gt; weapons and biological agents. In the past decade North Korea has become &gt; the world&#x27;s principal purveyor of ballistic missiles, and has tested &gt; increasingly capable missiles while developing its own WMD arsenal. &gt; Other rogue regimes seek nuclear, biological, and chemical weapons as &gt; well. These states&#x27; pursuit of, and global trade in, such weapons has &gt; become a looming threat to all nations. &gt; &gt; We must be prepared to stop rogue states and their terrorist clients &gt; before they are able to threaten or use weapons of mass destruction &gt; against the United States and our allies and friends. Our response must &gt; take full advantage of strengthened alliances, the establishment of new &gt; partnerships with former adversaries, innovation in the use of military &gt; forces, modern technologies, including the development of an effective &gt; missile defense system, and increased emphasis on intelligence &gt; collection and analysis. &gt; &gt; Our comprehensive strategy to combat WMD includes: &gt; &gt; * Proactive counterproliferation efforts. We must deter and defend &gt; against the threat before it is unleashed. We must ensure that key &gt; capabilities -- detection, active and passive defenses, and counterforce &gt; capabilities -- are integrated into our defense transformation and our &gt; homeland security systems. Counterproliferation must also be integrated &gt; into the doctrine, training, and equipping of our forces and those of &gt; our allies to ensure that we can prevail in any conflict with WMD-armed &gt; adversaries. &gt; &gt; * Strengthened nonproliferation efforts to prevent rogue states and &gt; terrorists from acquiring the materials, technologies and expertise &gt; necessary for weapons of mass destruction. We will enhance diplomacy, &gt; arms control, multilateral export controls, and threat reduction &gt; assistance that impede states and terrorists seeking WMD, and when &gt; necessary, interdict enabling technologies and materials. We will &gt; continue to build coalitions to support these efforts, encouraging their &gt; increased political and financial support for nonproliferation and &gt; threat reduction programs. The recent G-8 agreement to commit up to $20 &gt; billion to a global partnership against proliferation marks a major step &gt; forward. &gt; &gt; * Effective consequence management to respond to the effects of WMD &gt; use, whether by terrorists or hostile states. Minimizing the effects of &gt; WMD use against our people will help deter those who possess such &gt; weapons and dissuade those who seek to acquire them by persuading &gt; enemies that they cannot attain their desired ends. The United States &gt; must also be prepared to respond to the effects of WMD use against our &gt; forces abroad, and to help friends and allies if they are attacked. &gt; &gt; &gt; It has taken almost a decade for us to comprehend the true nature of &gt; this new threat. Given the goals of rogue states and terrorists, the &gt; United States can no longer solely rely on a reactive posture as we have &gt; in the past. The inability to deter a potential attacker, the immediacy &gt; of today&#x27;s threats, and the magnitude of potential harm that could be &gt; caused by our adversaries&#x27; choice of weapons, do not permit that option. &gt; We cannot let our enemies strike first. &gt; &gt; * In the Cold War, especially following the Cuban missile crisis, we &gt; faced a generally status quo, risk-averse adversary. Deterrence was an &gt; effective defense. But deterrence based only upon the threat of &gt; retaliation is far less likely to work against leaders of rogue states &gt; more willing to take risks, gambling with the lives of their people, and &gt; the wealth of their nations. &gt; &gt; * In the Cold War, weapons of mass destruction were considered &gt; weapons of last resort whose use risked the destruction of those who &gt; used them. Today, our enemies see weapons of mass destruction as weapons &gt; of choice. For rogue states these weapons are tools of intimidation and &gt; military aggression against their neighbors. These weapons may also &gt; allow these states to attempt to blackmail the United States and our &gt; allies to prevent us from deterring or repelling the aggressive behavior &gt; of rogue states. Such states also see these weapons as their best means &gt; of overcoming the conventional superiority of the United States. &gt; &gt; * Traditional concepts of deterrence will not work against a &gt; terrorist enemy whose avowed tactics are wanton destruction and the &gt; targeting of innocents; whose so-called soldiers seek martyrdom in death &gt; and whose most potent protection is statelessness. The overlap between &gt; states that sponsor terror and those that pursue WMD compels us to &gt; action. &gt; &gt; &gt; For centuries, international law recognized that nations need not suffer &gt; an attack before they can lawfully take action to defend themselves &gt; against forces that present an imminent danger of attack. Legal scholars &gt; and international jurists often conditioned the legitimacy of preemption &gt; on the existence of an imminent threat -- most often a visible &gt; mobilization of armies, navies, and air forces preparing to attack. &gt; &gt; We must adapt the concept of imminent threat to the capabilities and &gt; objectives of today&#x27;s adversaries. Rogue states and terrorists do not &gt; seek to attack us using conventional means. They know such attacks would &gt; fail. Instead, they rely on acts of terrorism and, potentially, the use &gt; of weapons of mass destruction -- weapons that can be easily concealed &gt; and delivered covertly and without warning. &gt; &gt; The targets of these attacks are our military forces and our civilian &gt; population, in direct violation of one of the principal norms of the law &gt; of warfare. As was demonstrated by the losses on September 11, 2001, &gt; mass civilian casualties is the specific objective of terrorists and &gt; these losses would be exponentially more severe if terrorists acquired &gt; and used weapons of mass destruction. &gt; &gt; The United States has long maintained the option of preemptive actions &gt; to counter a sufficient threat to our national security. The greater the &gt; threat, the greater is the risk of inaction -- and the more compelling &gt; the case for taking anticipatory action to defend ourselves, even if &gt; uncertainty remains as to the time and place of the enemy&#x27;s attack. To &gt; forestall or prevent such hostile acts by our adversaries, the United &gt; States will, if necessary, act preemptively. &gt; &gt; The United States will not use force in all cases to preempt emerging &gt; threats, nor should nations use preemption as a pretext for aggression. &gt; Yet in an age where the enemies of civilization openly and actively seek &gt; the world&#x27;s most destructive technologies, the United States cannot &gt; remain idle while dangers gather. &gt; &gt; We will always proceed deliberately, weighing the consequences of our &gt; actions. To support preemptive options, we will: &gt; &gt; * build better, more integrated intelligence capabilities to provide &gt; timely, accurate information on threats, wherever they may emerge; &gt; &gt; * coordinate closely with allies to form a common assessment of the &gt; most dangerous threats; and &gt; &gt; * continue to transform our military forces to ensure our ability to &gt; conduct rapid and precise operations to achieve decisive results. &gt; &gt; &gt; The purpose of our actions will always be to eliminate a specific threat &gt; to the United States or our allies and friends. The reasons for our &gt; actions will be clear, the force measured, and the cause just. &gt; &gt; VI. Ignite a New Era of Global Economic Growth through Free Markets and &gt; Free Trade. &gt; &gt; &gt; &quot;When nations close their markets and opportunity is hoarded by a &gt; privileged few, no amount -- no amount -- of development aid is ever &gt; enough. When nations respect their people, open markets, invest in &gt; better health and education, every dollar of aid, every dollar of trade &gt; revenue and domestic capital is used more effectively.&quot; &gt; &gt; &gt; &gt; &gt; &gt; President Bush &gt; Monterrey, Mexico &gt; March 22, 2002 &gt; &gt; &gt; &gt; A strong world economy enhances our national security by advancing &gt; prosperity and freedom in the rest of the world. Economic growth &gt; supported by free trade and free markets creates new jobs and higher &gt; incomes. It allows people to lift their lives out of poverty, spurs &gt; economic and legal reform, and the fight against corruption, and it &gt; reinforces the habits of liberty. &gt; &gt; We will promote economic growth and economic freedom beyond America&#x27;s &gt; shores. All governments are responsible for creating their own economic &gt; policies and responding to their own economic challenge. We will use our &gt; economic engagement with other countries to underscore the benefits of &gt; policies that generate higher productivity and sustained economic &gt; growth, including: &gt; &gt; * pro-growth legal and regulatory policies to encourage business &gt; investment, innovation, and entrepreneurial activity; &gt; &gt; * tax policies -- particularly lower marginal tax rates -- that &gt; improve incentives for work and investment; &gt; &gt; * rule of law and intolerance of corruption so that people are &gt; confident that they will be able to enjoy the fruits of their economic &gt; endeavors; &gt; &gt; * strong financial systems that allow capital to be put to its most &gt; efficient use; &gt; &gt; * sound fiscal policies to support business activity; &gt; &gt; * investments in health and education that improve the well-being and &gt; skills of the labor force and population as a whole; and &gt; &gt; * free trade that provides new avenues for growth and fosters the &gt; diffusion of technologies and ideas that increase productivity and &gt; opportunity. &gt; &gt; &gt; The lessons of history are clear: market economies, not &gt; command-and-control economies with the heavy hand of government, are the &gt; best way to promote prosperity and reduce poverty. Policies that further &gt; strengthen market incentives and market institutions are relevant for &gt; all economies -- industrialized countries, emerging markets, and the &gt; developing world. &gt; &gt; A return to strong economic growth in Europe and Japan is vital to U.S. &gt; national security interests. We want our allies to have strong economies &gt; for their own sake, for the sake of the global economy, and for the sake &gt; of global security. European efforts to remove structural barriers in &gt; their economies are particularly important in this regard, as are &gt; Japan&#x27;s efforts to end deflation and address the problems of &gt; non-performing loans in the Japanese banking system. We will continue to &gt; use our regular consultations with Japan and our European partners -- &gt; including through the Group of Seven (G-7) -- to discuss policies they &gt; are adopting to promote growth in their economies and support higher &gt; global economic growth. &gt; &gt; Improving stability in emerging markets is also key to global economic &gt; growth. International flows of investment capital are needed to expand &gt; the productive potential of these economies. These flows allow emerging &gt; markets and developing countries to make the investments that raise &gt; living standards and reduce poverty. Our long-term objective should be a &gt; world in which all countries have investment-grade credit ratings that &gt; allow them access to international capital markets and to invest in &gt; their future. &gt; &gt; We are committed to policies that will help emerging markets achieve &gt; access to larger capital flows at lower cost. To this end, we will &gt; continue to pursue reforms aimed at reducing uncertainty in financial &gt; markets. We will work actively with other countries, the International &gt; Monetary Fund (IMF), and the private sector to implement the G-7 Action &gt; Plan negotiated earlier this year for preventing financial crises and &gt; more effectively resolving them when they occur. &gt; &gt; The best way to deal with financial crises is to prevent them from &gt; occurring, and we have encouraged the IMF to improve its efforts doing &gt; so. We will continue to work with the IMF to streamline the policy &gt; conditions for its lending and to focus its lending strategy on &gt; achieving economic growth through sound fiscal and monetary policy, &gt; exchange rate policy, and financial sector policy. &gt; &gt; The concept of &quot;free trade&quot; arose as a moral principle even before it &gt; became a pillar of economics. If you can make something that others &gt; value, you should be able to sell it to them. If others make something &gt; that you value, you should be able to buy it. This is real freedom, the &gt; freedom for a person -- or a nation -- to make a living. To promote free &gt; trade, the Unites States has developed a comprehensive strategy: &gt; &gt; * Seize the global initiative. The new global trade negotiations we &gt; helped launch at Doha in November 2001 will have an ambitious agenda, &gt; especially in agriculture, manufacturing, and services, targeted for &gt; completion in 2005. The United States has led the way in completing the &gt; accession of China and a democratic Taiwan to the World Trade &gt; Organization. We will assist Russia&#x27;s preparations to join the WTO. &gt; &gt; * Press regional initiatives. The United States and other democracies &gt; in the Western Hemisphere have agreed to create the Free Trade Area of &gt; the Americas, targeted for completion in 2005. This year the United &gt; States will advocate market-access negotiations with its partners, &gt; targeted on agriculture, industrial goods, services, investment, and &gt; government procurement. We will also offer more opportunity to the &gt; poorest continent, Africa, starting with full use of the preferences &gt; allowed in the African Growth and Opportunity Act, and leading to free &gt; trade. &gt; &gt; * Move ahead with bilateral free trade agreements. Building on the &gt; free trade agreement with Jordan enacted in 2001, the Administration &gt; will work this year to complete free trade agreements with Chile and &gt; Singapore. Our aim is to achieve free trade agreements with a mix of &gt; developed and developing countries in all regions of the world. &gt; Initially, Central America, Southern Africa, Morocco, and Australia will &gt; be our principal focal points. &gt; &gt; * Renew the executive-congressional partnership. Every &gt; administration&#x27;s trade strategy depends on a productive partnership with &gt; Congress. After a gap of 8 years, the Administration reestablished &gt; majority support in the Congress for trade liberalization by passing &gt; Trade Promotion Authority and the other market opening measures for &gt; developing countries in the Trade Act of 2002. This Administration will &gt; work with Congress to enact new bilateral, regional, and global trade &gt; agreements that will be concluded under the recently passed Trade &gt; Promotion Authority. &gt; &gt; * Promote the connection between trade and development. Trade &gt; policies can help developing countries strengthen property rights, &gt; competition, the rule of law, investment, the spread of knowledge, open &gt; societies, the efficient allocation of resources, and regional &gt; integration -- all leading to growth, opportunity, and confidence in &gt; developing countries. The United States is implementing The Africa &gt; Growth and Opportunity Act to provide market-access for nearly all goods &gt; produced in the 35 countries of sub-Saharan Africa. We will make more &gt; use of this act and its equivalent for the Caribbean Basin and continue &gt; to work with multilateral and regional institutions to help poorer &gt; countries take advantage of these opportunities. Beyond market access, &gt; the most important area where trade intersects with poverty is in public &gt; health. We will ensure that the WTO intellectual property rules are &gt; flexible enough to allow developing nations to gain access to critical &gt; medicines for extraordinary dangers like HIV/AIDS, tuberculosis, and &gt; malaria. &gt; &gt; * Enforce trade agreements and laws against unfair practices. &gt; Commerce depends on the rule of law; international trade depends on &gt; enforceable agreements. Our top priorities are to resolve ongoing &gt; disputes with the European Union, Canada, and Mexico and to make a &gt; global effort to address new technology, science, and health regulations &gt; that needlessly impede farm exports and improved agriculture. Laws &gt; against unfair trade practices are often abused, but the international &gt; community must be able to address genuine concerns about government &gt; subsidies and dumping. International industrial espionage which &gt; undermines fair competition must be detected and deterred. &gt; &gt; * Help domestic industries and workers adjust. There is a sound &gt; statutory framework for these transitional safeguards which we have used &gt; in the agricultural sector and which we are using this year to help the &gt; American steel industry. The benefits of free trade depend upon the &gt; enforcement of fair trading practices. These safeguards help ensure that &gt; the benefits of free trade do not come at the expense of American &gt; workers. Trade adjustment assistance will help workers adapt to the &gt; change and dynamism of open markets. &gt; &gt; * Protect the environment and workers. The United States must foster &gt; economic growth in ways that will provide a better life along with &gt; widening prosperity. We will incorporate labor and environmental &gt; concerns into U.S. trade negotiations, creating a healthy &quot;network&quot; &gt; between multilateral environmental agreements with the WTO, and use the &gt; International Labor Organization, trade preference programs, and trade &gt; talks to improve working conditions in conjunction with freer trade. &gt; &gt; * Enhance energy security. We will strengthen our own energy security &gt; and the shared prosperity of the global economy by working with our &gt; allies, trading partners, and energy producers to expand the sources and &gt; types of global energy supplied, especially in the Western Hemisphere, &gt; Africa, Central Asia, and the Caspian region. We will also continue to &gt; work with our partners to develop cleaner and more energy efficient &gt; technologies. &gt; &gt; &gt; Economic growth should be accompanied by global efforts to stabilize &gt; greenhouse gas concentrations associated with this growth, containing &gt; them at a level that prevents dangerous human interference with the &gt; global climate. Our overall objective is to reduce America&#x27;s greenhouse &gt; gas emissions relative to the size of our economy, cutting such &gt; emissions per unit of economic activity by 18 percent over the next 10 &gt; years, by the year 2012. Our strategies for attaining this goal will be &gt; to: &gt; &gt; * remain committed to the basic U.N. Framework Convention for &gt; international cooperation; &gt; &gt; * obtain agreements with key industries to cut emissions of some of &gt; the most potent greenhouse gases and give transferable credits to &gt; companies that can show real cuts; &gt; &gt; * develop improved standards for measuring and registering emission &gt; reductions; &gt; &gt; * promote renewable energy production and clean coal technology, as &gt; well as nuclear power -- which produces no greenhouse gas emissions, &gt; while also improving fuel economy for U.S. cars and trucks; &gt; &gt; * increase spending on research and new conservation technologies, to &gt; a total of $4.5 billion -- the largest sum being spent on climate change &gt; by any country in the world and a $700 million increase over last year&#x27;s &gt; budget; and &gt; &gt; * assist developing countries, especially the major greenhouse gas &gt; emitters such as China and India, so that they will have the tools and &gt; resources to join this effort and be able to grow along a cleaner and &gt; better path. &gt; &gt; &gt; VII. Expand the Circle of Development by Opening Societies and Building &gt; the Infrastructure of Democracy &gt; &gt; &gt; &quot;In World War II we fought to make the world safer, then worked to &gt; rebuild it. As we wage war today to keep the world safe from terror, we &gt; must also work to make the world a better place for all its citizens.&quot; &gt; &gt; &gt; &gt; &gt; &gt; President Bush &gt; Washington, D.C. (Inter-American &gt; Development Bank) &gt; March 14, 2002 &gt; &gt; &gt; &gt; A world where some live in comfort and plenty, while half of the human &gt; race lives on less than $2 a day, is neither just nor stable. Including &gt; all of the world&#x27;s poor in an expanding circle of development -- and &gt; opportunity -- is a moral imperative and one of the top priorities of &gt; U.S. international policy. &gt; &gt; Decades of massive development assistance have failed to spur economic &gt; growth in the poorest countries. Worse, development aid has often served &gt; to prop up failed policies, relieving the pressure for reform and &gt; perpetuating misery. Results of aid are typically measured in dollars &gt; spent by donors, not in the rates of growth and poverty reduction &gt; achieved by recipients. These are the indicators of a failed strategy. &gt; &gt; Working with other nations, the United States is confronting this &gt; failure. We forged a new consensus at the U.N. Conference on Financing &gt; for Development in Monterrey that the objectives of assistance -- and &gt; the strategies to achieve those objectives -- must change. &gt; &gt; This Administration&#x27;s goal is to help unleash the productive potential &gt; of individuals in all nations. Sustained growth and poverty reduction is &gt; impossible without the right national policies. Where governments have &gt; implemented real policy changes we will provide significant new levels &gt; of assistance. The United States and other developed countries should &gt; set an ambitious and specific target: to double the size of the world&#x27;s &gt; poorest economies within a decade. &gt; &gt; The United States Government will pursue these major strategies to &gt; achieve this goal: &gt; &gt; * Provide resources to aid countries that have met the challenge of &gt; national reform. We propose a 50 percent increase in the core &gt; development assistance given by the United States. While continuing our &gt; present programs, including humanitarian assistance based on need alone, &gt; these billions of new dollars will form a new Millennium Challenge &gt; Account for projects in countries whose governments rule justly, invest &gt; in their people, and encourage economic freedom. Governments must fight &gt; corruption, respect basic human rights, embrace the rule of law, invest &gt; in health care and education, follow responsible economic policies, and &gt; enable entrepreneurship. The Millennium Challenge Account will reward &gt; countries that have demonstrated real policy change and challenge those &gt; that have not to implement reforms. &gt; &gt; * Improve the effectiveness of the World Bank and other development &gt; banks in raising living standards. The United States is committed to a &gt; comprehensive reform agenda for making the World Bank and the other &gt; multilateral development banks more effective in improving the lives of &gt; the world&#x27;s poor. We have reversed the downward trend in U.S. &gt; contributions and proposed an 18 percent increase in the U.S. &gt; contributions to the International Development Association (IDA) -- the &gt; World Bank&#x27;s fund for the poorest countries -- and the African &gt; Development Fund. The key to raising living standards and reducing &gt; poverty around the world is increasing productivity growth, especially &gt; in the poorest countries. We will continue to press the multilateral &gt; development banks to focus on activities that increase economic &gt; productivity, such as improvements in education, health, rule of law, &gt; and private sector development. Every project, every loan, every grant &gt; must be judged by how much it will increase productivity growth in &gt; developing countries. &gt; &gt; * Insist upon measurable results to ensure that development &gt; assistance is actually making a difference in the lives of the world&#x27;s &gt; poor. When it comes to economic development, what really matters is that &gt; more children are getting a better education, more people have access to &gt; health care and clean water, or more workers can find jobs to make a &gt; better future for their families. We have a moral obligation to measure &gt; the success of our development assistance by whether it is delivering &gt; results. For this reason, we will continue to demand that our own &gt; development assistance as well as assistance from the multilateral &gt; development banks has measurable goals and concrete benchmarks for &gt; achieving those goals. Thanks to U.S. leadership, the recent IDA &gt; replenishment agreement will establish a monitoring and evaluation &gt; system that measures recipient countries&#x27; progress. For the first time, &gt; donors can link a portion of their contributions to IDA to the &gt; achievement of actual development results, and part of the U.S. &gt; contribution is linked in this way. We will strive to make sure that the &gt; World Bank and other multilateral development banks build on this &gt; progress so that a focus on results is an integral part of everything &gt; that these institutions do. &gt; &gt; * Increase the amount of development assistance that is provided in &gt; the form of grants instead of loans. Greater use of results-based grants &gt; is the best way to help poor countries make productive investments, &gt; particularly in the social sectors, without saddling them with &gt; ever-larger debt burdens. As a result of U.S. leadership, the recent IDA &gt; agreement provided for significant increases in grant funding for the &gt; poorest countries for education, HIV/AIDS, health, nutrition, water, &gt; sanitation, and other human needs. Our goal is to build on that progress &gt; by increasing the use of grants at the other multilateral development &gt; banks. We will also challenge universities, nonprofits, and the private &gt; sector to match government efforts by using grants to support &gt; development projects that show results. &gt; &gt; * Open societies to commerce and investment. Trade and investment are &gt; the real engines of economic growth. Even if government aid increases, &gt; most money for development must come from trade, domestic capital, and &gt; foreign investment. An effective strategy must try to expand these flows &gt; as well. Free markets and free trade are key priorities of our national &gt; security strategy. &gt; &gt; * Secure public health. The scale of the public health crisis in poor &gt; countries is enormous. In countries afflicted by epidemics and pandemics &gt; like HIV/AIDS, malaria, and tuberculosis, growth and development will be &gt; threatened until these scourges can be contained. Resources from the &gt; developed world are necessary but will be effective only with honest &gt; governance, which supports prevention programs and provides effective &gt; local infrastructure. The United States has strongly backed the new &gt; global fund for HIV/AIDS organized by U.N. Secretary General Kofi Annan &gt; and its focus on combining prevention with a broad strategy for &gt; treatment and care. The United States already contributes more than &gt; twice as much money to such efforts as the next largest donor. If the &gt; global fund demonstrates its promise, we will be ready to give even more. &gt; &gt; * Emphasize education. Literacy and learning are the foundation of &gt; democracy and development. Only about 7 percent of World Bank resources &gt; are devoted to education. This proportion should grow. The United States &gt; will increase its own funding for education assistance by at least 20 &gt; percent with an emphasis on improving basic education and teacher &gt; training in Africa. The United States can also bring information &gt; technology to these societies, many of whose education systems have been &gt; devastated by AIDS. &gt; &gt; * Continue to aid agricultural development. New technologies, &gt; including biotechnology, have enormous potential to improve crop yields &gt; in developing countries while using fewer pesticides and less water. &gt; Using sound science, the United States should help bring these benefits &gt; to the 800 million people, including 300 million children, who still &gt; suffer from hunger and malnutrition. &gt; &gt; &gt; VIII. Develop Agendas for Cooperative Action with the Other Main Centers &gt; of Global Power &gt; &gt; &gt; &quot;We have our best chance since the rise of the nation-state in the 17th &gt; century to build a world where the great powers compete in peace instead &gt; of prepare for war.&quot; &gt; &gt; &gt; &gt; &gt; &gt; President Bush &gt; West Point, New York &gt; June 1, 2002 &gt; &gt; &gt; &gt; America will implement its strategies by organizing coalitions -- as &gt; broad as practicable -- of states able and willing to promote a balance &gt; of power that favors freedom. Effective coalition leadership requires &gt; clear priorities, an appreciation of others&#x27; interests, and consistent &gt; consultations among partners with a spirit of humility. &gt; &gt; There is little of lasting consequence that the United States can &gt; accomplish in the world without the sustained cooperation of its allies &gt; and friends in Canada and Europe. Europe is also the seat of two of the &gt; strongest and most able international institutions in the world: the &gt; North Atlantic Treaty Organization (NATO), which has, since its &gt; inception, been the fulcrum of transatlantic and inter-European &gt; security, and the European Union (EU), our partner in opening world &gt; trade. &gt; &gt; The attacks of September 11 were also an attack on NATO, as NATO itself &gt; recognized when it invoked its Article V self-defense clause for the &gt; first time. NATO&#x27;s core mission -- collective defense of the &gt; transatlantic alliance of democracies -- remains, but NATO must develop &gt; new structures and capabilities to carry out that mission under new &gt; circumstances. NATO must build a capability to field, at short notice, &gt; highly mobile, specially trained forces whenever they are needed to &gt; respond to a threat against any member of the alliance. &gt; &gt; The alliance must be able to act wherever our interests are threatened, &gt; creating coalitions under NATO&#x27;s own mandate, as well as contributing to &gt; mission-based coalitions. To achieve this, we must: &gt; &gt; * expand NATO&#x27;s membership to those democratic nations willing and &gt; able to share the burden of defending and advancing our common interests; &gt; &gt; * ensure that the military forces of NATO nations have appropriate &gt; combat contributions to make in coalition warfare; &gt; &gt; * develop planning processes to enable those contributions to become &gt; effective multinational fighting forces; &gt; &gt; * take advantage of the technological opportunities and economies of &gt; scale in our defense spending to transform NATO military forces so that &gt; they dominate potential aggressors and diminish our vulnerabilities; &gt; &gt; * streamline and increase the flexibility of command structures to &gt; meet new operational demands and the associated requirements of &gt; training, integrating, and experimenting with new force configurations; &gt; and &gt; &gt; * maintain the ability to work and fight together as allies even as &gt; we take the necessary steps to transform and modernize our forces. &gt; &gt; &gt; If NATO succeeds in enacting these changes, the rewards will be a &gt; partnership as central to the security and interests of its member &gt; states as was the case during the Cold War. We will sustain a common &gt; perspective on the threats to our societies and improve our ability to &gt; take common action in defense of our nations and their interests. At the &gt; same time, we welcome our European allies&#x27; efforts to forge a greater &gt; foreign policy and defense identity with the EU, and commit ourselves to &gt; close consultations to ensure that these developments work with NATO. We &gt; cannot afford to lose this opportunity to better prepare the family of &gt; transatlantic democracies for the challenges to come. &gt; &gt; The attacks of September 11 energized America&#x27;s Asian alliances. &gt; Australia invoked the ANZUS Treaty to declare the September 11 was an &gt; attack on Australia itself, following that historic decision with the &gt; dispatch of some of the world&#x27;s finest combat forces for Operation &gt; Enduring Freedom. Japan and the Republic of Korea provided unprecedented &gt; levels of military logistical support within weeks of the terrorist &gt; attack. We have deepened cooperation on counter-terrorism with our &gt; alliance partners in Thailand and the Philippines and received &gt; invaluable assistance from close friends like Singapore and New Zealand. &gt; &gt; The war against terrorism has proven that America&#x27;s alliances in Asia &gt; not only underpin regional peace and stability, but are flexible and &gt; ready to deal with new challenges. To enhance our Asian alliances and &gt; friendships, we will: &gt; &gt; &gt; * look to Japan to continue forging a leading role in regional and &gt; global affairs based on our common interests, our common values, and our &gt; close defense and diplomatic cooperation; &gt; &gt; * work with South Korea to maintain vigilance towards the North while &gt; preparing our alliance to make contributions to the broader stability of &gt; the region over the longer-term; &gt; &gt; * build on 50 years of U.S.-Australian alliance cooperation as we &gt; continue working together to resolve regional and global problems -- as &gt; we have so many times from the Battle of Leyte Gulf to Tora Bora; &gt; &gt; * maintain forces in the region that reflect our commitments to our &gt; allies, our requirements, our technological advances, and the strategic &gt; environment; and &gt; &gt; * build on stability provided by these alliances, as well as with &gt; institutions such as ASEAN and the Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation &gt; forum, to develop a mix of regional and bilateral strategies to manage &gt; change in this dynamic region. &gt; &gt; &gt; We are attentive to the possible renewal of old patterns of great power &gt; competition. Several potential great powers are now in the midst of &gt; internal transition -- most importantly Russia, India, and China. In all &gt; three cases, recent developments have encouraged our hope that a truly &gt; global consensus about basic principles is slowly taking shape. &gt; &gt; With Russia, we are already building a new strategic relationship based &gt; on a central reality of the twenty-first century: the United States and &gt; Russia are no longer strategic adversaries. The Moscow Treaty on &gt; Strategic Reductions is emblematic of this new reality and reflects a &gt; critical change in Russian thinking that promises to lead to productive, &gt; long-term relations with the Euro-Atlantic community and the United &gt; States. Russia&#x27;s top leaders have a realistic assessment of their &gt; country&#x27;s current weakness and the policies -- internal and external -- &gt; needed to reverse those weaknesses. They understand, increasingly, that &gt; Cold War approaches do not serve their national interests and that &gt; Russian and American strategic interests overlap in many areas. &gt; &gt; United States policy seeks to use this turn in Russian thinking to &gt; refocus our relationship on emerging and potential common interests and &gt; challenges. We are broadening our already extensive cooperation in the &gt; global war on terrorism. We are facilitating Russia&#x27;s entry into the &gt; World Trade Organization, without lowering standards for accession, to &gt; promote beneficial bilateral trade and investment relations. We have &gt; created the NATO-Russia Council with the goal of deepening security &gt; cooperation among Russia, our European allies, and ourselves. We will &gt; continue to bolster the independence and stability of the states of the &gt; former Soviet Union in the belief that a prosperous and stable &gt; neighborhood will reinforce Russia&#x27;s growing commitment to integration &gt; into the Euro-Atlantic community. &gt; &gt; At the same time, we are realistic about the differences that still &gt; divide us from Russia and about the time and effort it will take to &gt; build an enduring strategic partnership. Lingering distrust of our &gt; motives and policies by key Russian elites slows improvement in our &gt; relations. Russia&#x27;s uneven commitment to the basic values of free-market &gt; democracy and dubious record in combating the proliferation of weapons &gt; of mass destruction remain matters of great concern. Russia&#x27;s very &gt; weakness limits the opportunities for cooperation. Nevertheless, those &gt; opportunities are vastly greater now than in recent years -- or even &gt; decades. &gt; &gt; The United States has undertaken a transformation in its bilateral &gt; relationship with India based on a conviction that U.S. interests &gt; require a strong relationship with India. We are the two largest &gt; democracies, committed to political freedom protected by representative &gt; government. India is moving toward greater economic freedom as well. We &gt; have a common interest in the free flow of commerce, including through &gt; the vital sea lanes of the Indian Ocean. Finally, we share an interest &gt; in fighting terrorism and in creating a strategically stable Asia. &gt; &gt; Differences remain, including over the development of India&#x27;s nuclear &gt; and missile programs, and the pace of India&#x27;s economic reforms. But &gt; while in the past these concerns may have dominated our thinking about &gt; India, today we start with a view of India as a growing world power with &gt; which we have common strategic interests. Through a strong partnership &gt; with India, we can best address any differences and shape a dynamic &gt; future. &gt; &gt; The United States relationship with China is an important part of our &gt; strategy to promote a stable, peaceful, and prosperous Asia-Pacific &gt; region. We welcome the emergence of a strong, peaceful, and prosperous &gt; China. The democratic development of China is crucial to that future. &gt; Yet, a quarter century after beginning the process of shedding the worst &gt; features of the Communist legacy, China&#x27;s leaders have not yet made the &gt; next series of fundamental choices about the character of their state. &gt; In pursuing advanced military capabilities that can threaten its &gt; neighbors in the Asia-Pacific region, China is following an outdated &gt; path that, in the end, will hamper its own pursuit of national &gt; greatness. In time, China will find that social and political freedom is &gt; the only source of that greatness. &gt; &gt; The United States seeks a constructive relationship with a changing &gt; China. We already cooperate well where our interests overlap, including &gt; the current war on terrorism and in promoting stability on the Korean &gt; peninsula. Likewise, we have coordinated on the future of Afghanistan &gt; and have initiated a comprehensive dialogue on counter-terrorism and &gt; similar transitional concerns. Shared health and environmental threats, &gt; such as the spread of HIV/AIDS, challenge us to promote jointly the &gt; welfare of our citizens. &gt; &gt; Addressing these transnational threats will challenge China to become &gt; more open with information, promote the development of civil society, &gt; and enhance individual human rights. China has begun to take the road to &gt; political openness, permitting many personal freedoms and conducting &gt; village-level elections, yet remains strongly committed to national &gt; one-party rule by the Communist Party. To make that nation truly &gt; accountable to its citizen&#x27;s needs and aspirations, however, much work &gt; remains to be done. Only by allowing the Chinese people to think, &gt; assemble, and worship freely can China reach its full potential. &gt; &gt; Our important trade relationship will benefit from China&#x27;s entry into &gt; the World Trade Organization, which will create more export &gt; opportunities and ultimately more jobs for American farmers, workers, &gt; and companies. China is our fourth largest trading partner, with over &gt; $100 billion in annual two-way trade. The power of market principles and &gt; the WTO&#x27;s requirements for transparency and accountability will advance &gt; openness and the rule of law in China to help establish basic &gt; protections for commerce and for citizens. There are, however, other &gt; areas in which we have profound disagreements. Our commitment to the &gt; self-defense of Taiwan under the Taiwan Relations Act is one. Human &gt; rights is another. We expect China to adhere to its nonproliferation &gt; commitments. We will work to narrow differences where they exist, but &gt; not allow them to preclude cooperation where we agree. &gt; &gt; The events of September 11, 2001, fundamentally changed the context for &gt; relations between the United States and other main centers of global &gt; power, and opened vast, new opportunities. With our long-standing allies &gt; in Europe and Asia, and with leaders in Russia, India, and China, we &gt; must develop active agendas of cooperation lest these relationships &gt; become routine and unproductive. &gt; &gt; Every agency of the United States Government shares the challenge. We &gt; can build fruitful habits of consultation, quiet argument, sober &gt; analysis, and common action. In the long-term, these are the practices &gt; that will sustain the supremacy of our common principles and keep open &gt; the path of progress. &gt; &gt; IX. Transform America&#x27;s National Security Institutions to Meet the &gt; Challenges and Opportunities of the Twenty-First Century &gt; &gt; &gt; &quot;Terrorists attacked a symbol of American prosperity. They did not touch &gt; its source. America is successful because of the hard work, creativity, &gt; and enterprise of our people.&quot; &gt; &gt; &gt; &gt; &gt; &gt; President Bush &gt; Washington, D.C. (Joint Session of Congress) &gt; September 20, 2001 &gt; &gt; &gt; &gt; The major institutions of American national security were designed in a &gt; different era to meet different requirements. All of them must be &gt; transformed. &gt; &gt; It is time to reaffirm the essential role of American military strength. &gt; We must build and maintain our defenses beyond challenge. Our military&#x27;s &gt; highest priority is to defend the United States. To do so effectively, &gt; our military must: &gt; &gt; * assure our allies and friends; &gt; &gt; * dissuade future military competition; &gt; &gt; * deter threats against U.S. interests, allies, and friends; and &gt; &gt; * decisively defeat any adversary if deterrence fails. &gt; &gt; &gt; The unparalleled strength of the United States armed forces, and their &gt; forward presence, have maintained the peace in some of the world&#x27;s most &gt; strategically vital regions. However, the threats and enemies we must &gt; confront have changed, and so must our forces. A military structured to &gt; deter massive Cold War-era armies must be transformed to focus more on &gt; how an adversary might fight rather than where and when a war might &gt; occur. We will channel our energies to overcome a host of operational &gt; challenges. &gt; &gt; The presence of American forces overseas is one of the most profound &gt; symbols of the U.S. commitments to allies and friends. Through our &gt; willingness to use force in our own defense and in defense of others, &gt; the United States demonstrates its resolve to maintain a balance of &gt; power that favors freedom. To contend with uncertainty and to meet the &gt; many security challenges we face, the United States will require bases &gt; and stations within and beyond Western Europe and Northeast Asia, as &gt; well as temporary access arrangements for the long-distance deployment &gt; of U.S. forces. &gt; &gt; Before the war in Afghanistan, that area was low on the list of major &gt; planning contingencies. Yet, in a very short time, we had to operate &gt; across the length and breadth of that remote nation, using every branch &gt; of the armed forces. We must prepare for more such deployments by &gt; developing assets such as advanced remote sensing, long-range precision &gt; strike capabilities, and transformed maneuver and expeditionary forces. &gt; This broad portfolio of military capabilities must also include the &gt; ability to defend the homeland, conduct information operations, ensure &gt; U.S. access to distant theaters, and protect critical U.S. &gt; infrastructure and assets in outer space. &gt; &gt; Innovation within the armed forces will rest on experimentation with new &gt; approaches to warfare, strengthening joint operations, exploiting U.S. &gt; intelligence advantages, and taking full advantage of science and &gt; technology. We must also transform the way the Department of Defense is &gt; run, especially in financial management and recruitment and retention. &gt; Finally, while maintaining near-term readiness and the ability to fight &gt; the war on terrorism, the goal must be to provide the President with a &gt; wider range of military options to discourage aggression or any form of &gt; coercion against the United States, our allies, and our friends. &gt; &gt; We know from history that deterrence can fail; and we know from &gt; experience that some enemies cannot be deterred. The United States must &gt; and will maintain the capability to defeat any attempt by an enemy -- &gt; whether a state or non-state actor -- to impose its will on the United &gt; States, our allies, or our friends. We will maintain the forces &gt; sufficient to support our obligations, and to defend freedom. Our forces &gt; will be strong enough to dissuade potential adversaries from pursuing a &gt; military build-up in hopes of surpassing, or equaling, the power of the &gt; United States. &gt; &gt; Intelligence -- and how we use it -- is our first line of defense &gt; against terrorists and the threat posed by hostile states. Designed &gt; around the priority of gathering enormous information about a massive, &gt; fixed object -- the Soviet bloc -- the intelligence community is coping &gt; with the challenge of following a far more complex and elusive set of &gt; targets. &gt; &gt; We must transform our intelligence capabilities and build new ones to &gt; keep pace with the nature of these threats. Intelligence must be &gt; appropriately integrated with our defense and law enforcement systems &gt; and coordinated with our allies and friends. We need to protect the &gt; capabilities we have so that we do not arm our enemies with the &gt; knowledge of how best to surprise us. Those who would harm us also seek &gt; the benefit of surprise to limit our prevention and response options and &gt; to maximize injury. &gt; &gt; We must strengthen intelligence warning and analysis to provide &gt; integrated threat assessments for national and homeland security. Since &gt; the threats inspired by foreign governments and groups may be conducted &gt; inside the United States, we must also ensure the proper fusion of &gt; information between intelligence and law enforcement. &gt; &gt; Initiatives in this area will include: &gt; &gt; * strengthening the authority of the Director of Central Intelligence &gt; to lead the development and actions of the Nation&#x27;s foreign intelligence &gt; capabilities; &gt; &gt; * establishing a new framework for intelligence warning that provides &gt; seamless and integrated warning across the spectrum of threats facing &gt; the nation and our allies; &gt; &gt; * continuing to develop new methods of collecting information to &gt; sustain our intelligence advantage; &gt; &gt; * investing in future capabilities while working to protect them &gt; through a more vigorous effort to prevent the compromise of intelligence &gt; capabilities; and &gt; &gt; * collecting intelligence against the terrorist danger across the &gt; government with all-source analysis. &gt; &gt; &gt; As the United States Government relies on the armed forces to defend &gt; America&#x27;s interests, it must rely on diplomacy to interact with other &gt; nations. We will ensure that the Department of State receives funding &gt; sufficient to ensure the success of American diplomacy. The State &gt; Department takes the lead in managing our bilateral relationships with &gt; other governments. And in this new era, its people and institutions must &gt; be able to interact equally adroitly with non-governmental organizations &gt; and international institutions. Officials trained mainly in &gt; international politics must also extend their reach to understand &gt; complex issues of domestic governance around the world, including public &gt; health, education, law enforcement, the judiciary, and public diplomacy. &gt; &gt; Our diplomats serve at the front line of complex negotiations, civil &gt; wars, and other humanitarian catastrophes. As humanitarian relief &gt; requirements are better understood, we must also be able to help build &gt; police forces, court systems, and legal codes, local and provincial &gt; government institutions, and electoral systems. Effective international &gt; cooperation is needed to accomplish these goals, backed by American &gt; readiness to play our part. &gt; &gt; Just as our diplomatic institutions must adapt so that we can reach out &gt; to others, we also need a different and more comprehensive approach to &gt; public information efforts that can help people around the world learn &gt; about and understand America. The war on terrorism is not a clash of &gt; civilizations. It does, however, reveal the clash inside a civilization, &gt; a battle for the future of the Muslim world. This is a struggle of ideas &gt; and this is an area where America must excel. &gt; &gt; We will take the actions necessary to ensure that our efforts to meet &gt; our global security commitments and protect Americans are not impaired &gt; by the potential for investigations, inquiry, or prosecution by the &gt; International Criminal Court (ICC), whose jurisdiction does not extend &gt; to Americans and which we do not accept. We will work together with &gt; other nations to avoid complications in our military operations and &gt; cooperation, through such mechanisms as multilateral and bilateral &gt; agreements that will protect U.S. nationals from the ICC. We will &gt; implement fully the American Servicemembers Protection Act, whose &gt; provisions are intended to ensure and enhance the protection of U.S. &gt; personnel and officials. &gt; &gt; We will make hard choices in the coming year and beyond to ensure the &gt; right level and allocation of government spending on national security. &gt; The United States Government must strengthen its defenses to win this &gt; war. At home, our most important priority is to protect the homeland for &gt; the American people. &gt; &gt; Today, the distinction between domestic and foreign affairs is &gt; diminishing. In a globalized world, events beyond America&#x27;s borders have &gt; a greater impact inside them. Our society must be open to people, ideas, &gt; and goods from across the globe. The characteristics we most cherish -- &gt; our freedom, our cities, our systems of movement, and modern life -- are &gt; vulnerable to terrorism. This vulnerability will persist long after we &gt; bring to justice those responsible for the September eleventh attacks. &gt; As time passes, individuals may gain access to means of destruction that &gt; until now could be wielded only by armies, fleets, and squadrons. This &gt; is a new condition of life. We will adjust to it and thrive -- in spite &gt; of it. &gt; &gt; In exercising our leadership, we will respect the values, judgment, and &gt; interests of our friends and partners. Still, we will be prepared to act &gt; apart when our interests and unique responsibilities require. When we &gt; disagree on particulars, we will explain forthrightly the grounds for &gt; our concerns and strive to forge viable alternatives. We will not allow &gt; such disagreements to obscure our determination to secure together, with &gt; our allies and our friends, our shared fundamental interests and values. &gt; &gt; Ultimately, the foundation of American strength is at home. It is in the &gt; skills of our people, the dynamism of our economy, and the resilience of &gt; our institutions. A diverse, modern society has inherent, ambitious, &gt; entrepreneurial energy. Our strength comes from what we do with that &gt; energy. That is where our national security begins. &gt;
</pre>

<pre>
books on the history of linguistics john benjamins publishing would like to call your attention to the following new titles in the history of linguistics : the emergence of semantics in four linguistic traditions hebrew , sanskrit , greek , arabic wout van bekkum , jan houben , ineke sluiter , kees versteegh 1997 ix , 322 pp . studies in the history of the language sciences , 82 us / canada : cloth : 1 55619 617 2 price : us $ 99 . 00 rest of the world : cloth : 90 272 4568 1 price : hfl . 198 , - - john benjamins publishing web site : http : / / www . benjamins . com for further information via e-mail : service @ benjamins . com the aim of this study is a comparative analysis of the role of semantics in the linguistic theory of four grammatical traditions , sanskrit , hebrew , greek , arabic . if one compares the organization of linguistic theory in various grammatical traditions , it soon turns out that there are marked differences in the way they define the place of &#x27;s emantics &#x27; within the theory . in some traditions , semantics is formally excluded from linguistic theory , and linguists do not express any opinion as to the relationship between syntactic and semantic analysis . in other traditions , the whole basis of linguistic theory is semantically orientated , and syntactic features are always analysed as correlates of a semantic structure . however , even in those traditions , in which semantics falls explicitly or implicitly outside the scope of linguistics , there may be factors forcing linguists to occupy themselves with the semantic dimension of language . one important factor seems to be the presence of a corpus of revealed / sacred texts : the necessity to formulate hermeneutic rules for the interpretation of this corpus brings semantics in through the back door . the noblest animate motion speech , pysiology and medicine in pre-cartesian linguistic thought jeffrey wollock 1997 xlvi , 470 pp . studies in the history of the language sciences , 83 us / canada : cloth : 1 55619 620 2 price : us $ 160 . 00 rest of the world : cloth : 90 272 4571 1 price : hfl . 320 , - - john benjamins publishing web site : http : / / www . benjamins . com for further information via e-mail : service @ benjamins . com the body of theory on speech production and speech disorder developed prior to descartes has been so neglected by historians that its very existence is practically unknown today . yet it provides a framework for understanding the speech process which is not only comprehensive and coherent , but of great relevance to current debates on issues of language performance and applied linguistics . this is because , the author contends , current theoretical difficulties stem largely from initial errors of descartes ; whereas earlier theoretical formulations , while outlining a bio-mechanics of speech , retain the central role of the human agent . the discussions explicated in this book come mainly from the natural - philosophic and medical literature of greco - roman antiquity , the middle ages , and the renaissance and early 17th century . this uncharted territory is mapped for the first time by tracing its textual history and diffusion as well as explaining the theory on its own terms but in language that will be clear and comprehensible to non-specialists . interdisciplinary in perspective , the book encompasses topics of interest not only to the language sciences , but also to the biosciences , medicine , philosophy of human movement , psychology and behavioral sciences , neurosciences , speech pathology , experimental phonetics , speech and rhetoric , and the history of science in general . - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - bernadette martinez - keck tel : ( 215 ) 836-1200 publicity / marketing fax : ( 215 ) 836-1204 john benjamins north america e-mail : bernie @ benjamins . com po box 27519 philadelphia pa 19118-0519 check out the john benjamins web site : http : / / www . benjamins . com
</pre>

## Record 009038

**Label:** `benign`

<pre>
I rigged this up for testing before I commit to a sitewide rollout of
Spamassassin as an SMTP relay.
It is more work, but it avoids sitewide changes in mail delivery during the
early stages of testing.On the Exchange server... (mail.yourdomain.com)
Create a new, private SMTP address (nomorespam@yourdomain.com) for the
mailbox you want to protect. Remove the old address.Create a &quot;custom recipient&quot; with the old SMTP address
(spammedalot@yourdomain.com). Create another custom recipient pointing at a
local user on the Spamassassin box (killspam@filtermail.yourdomain.com).Set the first custom recipient to forward all mail to the second custom
recipient. (must forward to another address in the Exchange directory)On the Spamassassin box... (filtermail.yourdomain.com)
Set up a dummy user, in this case &quot;killspam&quot;
Give killspam a .forward file pointing to the final recipient&#x27;s new, private
smtp address back on the Exchange server.So the message flow is like this:Mail arrives on the Exchange server mail.yourdomain.com for
spammedalot@yourdomain.comspammedalot@commonwealth.com forwards it to
killspam@filtermail.yourdomain.comFollowing the .forward file, the e-mail is then forwarded to
nomorespam@yourdomain.com
Wowie Zowie it works. The first address I protected was myself, the second
one I protected was postmaster.As you can see, you must create two custom recipients in Exchange and one
dummy account with a .forward file on the spam filter box for each address
you want to protect. Like I said, it is more work, but until I am done
load-testing, this is the way it will stay.-jake

## Record 009041

**Label:** `benign`

<pre>
tanlps workshop : final call for paper tanlps workshop : final call for papers held at the european conference on machine learning ( ecml &#x27; 98 ) , chemnitz , germany 21-24 april 1998 ( www address : http : / / www . tu-chemnitz . de / informatik / ecml98 / ) . tanlps towards adaptive nlp - driven systems : linguistic information , learning methods and applications 24 april 1998 organized by : r . basili , m . t . pazienza ( university of roma , tor vergata , italy ) since most of its applications , from syntactic to semantic , are lexicon driven , systematic and reliable acquisition on a large scale of linguistic information is the real challenge to natural language processing ( nlp ) . empiricist view on natural language processing and learning has become recently more attractive for a wider research community : computational linguistics , artificial intelligence , psychology then seemed to converge on a specific data-oriented perspective aiming to overcome the traditional knowledge acquisition bottleneck . it has been often noted that the limited attention paid by the machine learning community to text and speech data seems unjustified . it is thus more and more evident that empirical learning of natural language processing ( nlp ) can alleviate the nlp main problem by means of a variety of methods for the automatic induction of lexical knowledge . lexical knowledge is often hard to compile by hand , and even harder to port and reuse . nlp application systems still have a low impact on real world problems , mainly due to the costs related to reusability and customization of the required lexicons . in particular , changes in the domain causes changes in the lexical information required in the underlying natural language . empirical , symbolic machine learning methods can be perfectly suited for this task like automatic acquisition and adaptation of this knowledge . rule induction , symbolic approaches to clustering , lazy learning , and inductive logic programming , have been already proposed by a growing community that is entering the challenge for theoretical ( i . e . methodological ) and application purposes a variety of techniques seems to be combined in order to successfully design realistic inductive systems for text processing : the target of this research is to define methods and design principles for systems combining linguistic and lexical learning capabilities for large scale language processing tasks . this is what we mean with adaptive nlp - driven systems . within this research task , some issues can favour a synergistic process between nlp and ml areas : the access to large data sets , that are even increasing over time , due to the telematics facilities available nowadays ; extending the set of typical classes of ml problems to other hard cases ( particularly dense in the nlp processes ) ; adding inductive capabilities to nlp system for tasks related to specific applications ( i . e . information extraction ) . the proposed workshop thus aims at stimulating reasearch and discussion on the following aspects : - establishing results and evidencies on the suitability of different ml paradigms on specific levels of representation of lexical knowledge ( morphology , syntax , linguistic inference among others ) - comparison of the quantitative approaches to lexical acquisition with empirical symbolic methods - stimulating discussion on cognitive perspective of some models within a plausible architecture for language processing and learning - establishing results on the applicability of the extracted / induce knowledge within nlp systems , with respect to assessed evaluation criteria , typical of the ml and language engineering ( le ) area - case studies on adaptive nlp systems , i . e . effective nlp systems integrating linguistic inferences with inductive capabilities ( www kb at cmu , ecran , . . . ) , - critical review of existing experiences on adaptive nlp systems - establishing guidelines for an evaluation framework of adaptive nlp systems : accuracy of the linguistic process , robustness of the induction process , . . . - promote cooperation among research groups in europe and usa to exchange ideas , data and tools for design and experiment architectures for adaptive nlp systems related events a parallel workshop will be also held in the ecml conference : &quot; text mining &quot; organized by yves kodratoff . although close in intent and topics , the two workshops have a specific &quot; identity &quot; in terms of area of research , multidisciplinary aspects and contributions . so , in order to enhance the discussion and the synergistic contributions to the two independent approaches , submissions covering common problems ( i . e . adaptive data mining from textual data ) will undergo a specific review , in cooperation with the other workshop pc . in fact , a joint ( half day ) session on specific borderline topics as well as on existing ( hybrid ) systems has been planned and a subset of the accepted papers will be presented there . a particular effort will be asked to authors for stressing / enhancing synergistic aspects . the program committee will suggest guidelines to compare / generalize / extend individual contributions in this specific perspective . workshop format : the workshop is expected to cover the whole day . in the first session , apart from an invited talk , we expect to cover methodological issues . papers related to advanced research on suitability of learning paradigms for the different target lexical information will be favoured . prototypical examples in this area are studies on empirical learning of tasks like pos tagging , induction of grammatical information , symbolic learning of word sense disambiguation criteria and lexical semantic information . a panel discussion is expected to close the morning session and focus on principles of suitability for learning paradigms vs . lexical levels . in the second session we expect to stimulate participants to cover application areas , like ir and ie , on original research works that are currently under development in several research centres in europe ( sheffield , tilburg , rome tor vergata and torino universities ) . a panel discussion on the implication of the adaptive paradigm on existing and potential nlp systems will close the workshop . program committee r . basili ( university of roma , tor vergata , italy ) m . craven ( carnegie mellon university , usa ) w . daelemans ( university of tilburg , nederlands ) m . t . pazienza ( university of roma , tor vergata , italy ) l . saitta ( university of torino , italy ) c . samuelssonn ( bell labs , at&amp;t , usa ) y . wilks ( university of sheffield , uk ) paper Â  07.22.2002 GnomeREPORT Hackers and crackers respect only one thing: strong security.  Keep the bad guys out of your PC or network with the advanced technology that&#x27;s trusted by millions:  ZoneAlarm Pro 3.0. This best-in-class personal firewall stops hackers cold, defends your online privacy,and sets up in minutes. Click here to download now!   Listen to Lockergnomie Dale Lowery and you&#x27;ll save time
immediately. It&#x27;s an old tip, but it bears repeating: &quot;I&#x27;ll often
find Web snippets I&#x27;d like to keep - but I don&#x27;t want to save the
entire page. So, I&#x27;ve HOT-KEYED Notepad. Easy! Get to the most
common Notepad shortcut icon: Start | Programs | Accessories |
Notepad. Then right-click on the menu item and select Properties.
You&#x27;ll see that this is (indeed) a shortcut; in the lower part of
the dialog is a field to add a hotkey. I entered &#x27;N&#x27; for Notepad,
Windows offered CTRL and ALT as a prefix, and now I can launch
Notepad in an instant. I highlight the text I want to keep, copy
it, tap my shortcut, paste it, and then save the new text file
wherever I want. Most often, I use this trick to copy paragraphs
that describe downloads (and save them as text files in the same
folder as the download).&quot; Just make sure you don&#x27;t pick a keyboard
shortcut that&#x27;s already in use. Otherwise, one of them is gonna
get cranky.And while we&#x27;re taking a trip down memory lane, Lockergnomie Ian
Rich reminds us of Jouni Vuorio&#x27;s utility: The site is
jv16.org. The program is called
jv16 Power Tools, and this freeware is truly worthy of praise. It
combines a full-on registry management tool with several file
manager tools: one to hunt down temporary files in any format and
the other to help perform advanced file searches and comparisons.
Mainly, I wanted the program to be brought to the attention of the
consumer public, as it moves further and further along in
development it just keeps getting better. I&#x27;ve used it on windows
Me, and now XP - without crashes or hair-raising Registry
corruption. It backs up everything it changes and is very easy to
use. If you&#x27;re interested in something of shareware-quality with
forum support (for absolutely nothing), get this program. Anyway,
I love the newsletter and the work you guys and gals do at
Lockergnome. I hope that this author gets his recognition.&quot;
Consider this a huge nod!I love a good resource, and Lockergnomie Elton Jonsson from the
United Kingdom has one for us, too: &quot;As a dedicated subscriber, I
just had to tell about this one. Have you ever been driven mad
trying to find out the name of the piece of music on a television
advert? Well, at last, here in the UK, we have an answer to that
problem and, given that many multinationals use the same
advertising in the USA as they do here, it may help out you guys
as well. Check out Commercial
Breaks and Beats. I have no connection with it; I&#x27;m just
grateful that someone has had the brilliant idea of creating it.
If you check the message board on the site, you&#x27;ll see that I&#x27;m
not alone.&quot; This Google
Directory can help fill in the blanks, I&#x27;m sure. I
almost fell out of my chair the other day when I heard &quot;Yeh Yeh&quot;
(by TMBG) being played on a Chrysler advertisement. Way to go:
John, John, Dan, Dan, and Dan! You&#x27;re the dogs kids love to bite.And what&#x27;s say we round out the report with another tip?
Lockergnomie David Brown can oblige: &quot;The e-mail forwarding
service is a good idea, but you can do it yourself with Outlook
Express. Set up two accounts (one for your old account and one for
your new one). Craft your reply in a new message and save it.
Then, select Tools | Message Rules | Mail | New rule. Check &#x27;Where
the message is from specified account&#x27; in the first section.
&#x27;Reply with message&quot; in section two. Select the appropriate
account and message in the final section. Name it. You can also
have the messages flagged or moved to another folder if you want
to know which messages came from the old account. Once the rule
has been created, any mail from the old account will have a reply
sent. People should learn about mail rules, anyway.&quot; True! So,
what rule simplifies your day?See You in August,Â Â Â Â Â Â Â Â Â Â Â Â Â Â Chris PirilloÂ Â Â Â Â Â Â

Network File Monit 2.04QuicKalendar v1.0.2 [430k] W9x/2k/XP FREEhttp://www.trinfinitysoftware.com/quickalendar.shtml{Calendar utility} You say you want to get together on the 25th, but what DAY is that? I&#x27;m not good with calendar dates; truth be told, I don&#x27;t even know what day of the week it is most the time. If I didn&#x27;t have my PDA armed with a Calendar with me, then I wouldn&#x27;t show up to anything on time, let alone on the right day. Well, that is great for when I am mobile, but what about when I am in front of my PC? The PDA is docked, but that doesn&#x27;t mean I have to be without a calendar... enter QuicKalendar! &quot;QuicKalendar gives you quick and easy access to a calendar whenever you need it. You can view the calendar on screen, or print it out to jot down notes and appointments.&quot; You need to keep in mind that the print feature is relative to how you have QuicKalendar displayed on your machine. If you are in full screen mode, then the printout will be bigger than a standard sheet of letter paper. If you try printing in normal view, then the printout will be too small to write on. My suggestion is to play with the size of QuicKalendar&#x27;s screen until you get a printout that you can be proud of. Now, you&#x27;ll have no reason to be late for Gnomedex!
Diatris v1.0 [407k] W9x/2k/XP FREEhttp://www.webruler.com/mtreciokas/{Tetris-like game} I&#x27;ve always been fascinated with geometric shapes. To me everything has to be somewhat symmetrical... even my hair! So it was no wonder why I was addicted to playing the game Tetris for so many years. The only problem with Tetris is that, after awhile, it starts to get a little too easy. Diatris is a bundle of three different - and much more challenging - Tetris-like games. &quot;For the first game, Y-Diatris, the objects can move vertically and diagonally. The second game, X-Diatris, the objects can move only in two diagonal directions; the vertical drop is taken out. For the last game H-Diatris the objects can only move vertically and horizontally like most block drop games.&quot; I won&#x27;t kid you, these games can be pretty hard, but if you are a Tetris fan, then these set of games are for you. If you&#x27;re not a Tetris fan, don&#x27;t worry, there are a few other games you might like on this site, so check it out!
Recommend It!

How to Troubleshoot Issues with Reading CDshttp://support.microsoft.com/default.aspx?scid=kb;EN-US;Q321641{Disc device troubleshooting} &quot;This article describes how you can troubleshoot issues reading CD-ROM or DVD-ROM optical discs. This article also discusses how to troubleshoot common issues with CD-R and CD-RW devices. The steps in this article apply to compact disc (CD-ROM) drives, compact disc recordable (CD-R) drives, compact disc re-writable (CD-RW) drives, and digital video disc (DVD) drives.&quot; To summarize, you should try the steps on this site before calling your technical support. Some of the steps include closing unnecessary software, testing the disc in a different computer, and cleaning the disc. Make sure to clean a disc by wiping from the center of the disc outward... if you wipe the disc in a circular fashion, then you risk scratching it. The steps in this article will only serve to save you time, as you will probably be able to fix most disc problems by following the steps. If this article doesn&#x27;t help you resolve your disc problem, look on the bright side... you won&#x27;t have to spend so much time on the phone with technical support, because you&#x27;ll already know what the problem ISN&#x27;T! 
Recommend It!

    Unearthed by Sergei Polishchukhttp://www.modemspy.com/{Answering machine} &quot;Modem spy is a handy utility for phone calls recording. Features: 1) Records phone conversations. 2) Records from microphone. 3) Plays back recorded messages via phone line. 4) Plays back recorded messages via sound card. 5) Can record ALL incoming phone calls. 6) Has built-in software automatic gain control. 7) Detects and logs Caller ID.&quot; Gnomie Brian Stevens tried this one out for us, but had a small problem testing this on any of his machines... he doesn&#x27;t have any modems! Brian says, &quot;I was able to load the program, though, and try a few of the features out. You can record an audio memo to your machine, much like an answering machine. You can also e-mail this audio memo, although I don&#x27;t recommend you do that as the WAV file didn&#x27;t seem to play well when I tried e-mailing it to myself (which may have been due to the microphone it was recorded with). Other than that, Modem Spy is a solid program and is a great alternative to buying a traditional answering machine... plus in most cases it is cheaper!&quot; Keep in mind that, for Caller ID to work, you need the Caller ID service turned on by your phone service provider.
